\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{11}}{Representation of a beam being deformed under the displacement of the joints at its ends.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Fig. 11}}{\small Representation of a beam being deformed under the displacement of the joints at its ends.}}}\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Frame topology}
\par A frame representing a vehicle configuration $\kappa$ is completely characterized by its joint positions and the list of joint pairs linked by members.
\par\begin{definition}Given $\kappa=\{(\bm{p}_{i},\bm{\eta}_{i},\epsilon_{i})\ |\ i\in\llbracket 0;n-1\rrbracket\}$, the set of joints in the frame of $\kappa$ is given by
\begin{equation*}\bar{J}_{\kappa}:=\left\{\bm{p}_{i}+\bm{g}\ |\ i\in\llbracket 0;n-1\rrbracket,\bm{g}\in V^{\mathcal{D}}\right\}.\end{equation*}Note that $n^{\mathrm{jt}}:=\left|\bar{J}_{\kappa}\right|\leq 20n$, since there are $20$ joints per module and some joints are shared by multiple modules.
The $n^{\mathrm{jt}}$ joints can then be ordered arbitrarily into a list:
\begin{equation*}J_{\kappa}=\left[\bm{j}_{0},\dots,\bm{j}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\right],\end{equation*}such that $\bm{j}_{i}$ is the element of $\bar{J}_{\kappa}$ assigned to the index $i\in\llbracket 0;n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1\rrbracket$.
\end{definition}\par\begin{definition}Similarly, the members of the frame of $\kappa$ are defined by the list
\begin{equation}E_{\kappa}:=\left[(e_{0}^{-},e_{0}^{+}),(e_{1}^{-},e_{1}^{+}),\dots,(e_{n^{\mathrm{mb}}-1}^{-},e_{n^{\mathrm{mb}}-1}^{+})\right].\end{equation}$n^{\mathrm{mb}}$ is the number of members in the frame and for $i\in\llbracket 0;n^{\mathrm{mb}}-1\rrbracket$, $(e_{i}^{-},e_{i}^{+})\in\llbracket 0;n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1\rrbracket\times\llbracket 0;n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1\rrbracket$ is the pair of joint indices linked by member $i$, where the order of the joints in each pair is arbitrary.
Here, $n^{\mathrm{mb}}=30n$ since there are $30$ members per module and each member belongs to a single module.
\end{definition}\par\par\begin{definition}The frame of configuration $\kappa$ is defined by
\begin{equation}\mathcal{F}_{\kappa}:=\left(J_{\kappa},E_{\kappa}\right),\end{equation}where $J_{\kappa}$ and $E_{\kappa}$ are defined in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{def:structures:conf_frame_joints} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{def:structures:conf_frame_members}.
\end{definition}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Loads and displacements of frames}
\par When subject to constant external loads, a frame deforms until reaching equilibrium or failure.
Equilibrium is characterized by the deformation of the members, which, via elastic forces, compensate the external loads, as shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:structures:deformed_quad:max_compliance}.
\par Let the external forces and moments applied on frame $\mathcal{F}_{\kappa}$ be defined by $\left[(\bm{f}_{0},\bm{m}_{0}),\dots,(\bm{f}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1},\bm{m}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1})\right]$, where $(\bm{f}_{i},\bm{m}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}$ is applied at joint $i\in\llbracket 0;n^{\mathrm{mb}}-1\rrbracket$.
The resulting displacements of the joints at equilibrium are defined by the list $\left[(\bm{s}_{0},\bm{r}_{0}),\dots,(\bm{s}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1},\bm{r}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1})\right]$, where $\bm{s}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}$ is a translational displacement and $\bm{r}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}$ is the axis-angle representation of a rotation.
These displacements are assumed to be measured relative to the state of the unloaded frame at equilibrium.
With this definition of joint loads and displacements, the aggregated vectors of frame displacements and external loads are given by
\begin{equation}\bm{v}:=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{s}_{0}\\
\bm{r}_{0}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{s}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\\
\bm{r}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{6n^{\mathrm{jt}}},\ \bm{P}:=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{f}_{0}\\
\bm{m}_{0}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{f}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\\
\bm{m}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{6n^{\mathrm{jt}}}.\end{equation}\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Elastic stiffness}
\par In this work, a first-order approximation of the relationships between $\bm{v}$ and $\bm{P}$ via the \emph{elastic stiffness matrix} $\bm{K}_{\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}^{6n^{\mathrm{jt}}\times 6n^{\mathrm{jt}}}$ is used:
\begin{equation}\bm{P}=\bm{K}_{\kappa}\bm{v}.\end{equation}This method is appropriate for small displacements and provides a good approximation of a structure's behavior around equilibrium, allowing the comparison of modular configurations from structural performance indicators based on this method.
The linear relationship between displacements and external loads also allows the efficient structural optimization of modular configurations.
The procedure to construct the elastic stiffness matrix of a space frame, which is a symmetric positive semi-definite, can be found in any comprehensive textbook on matrix methods for structural analysis~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{weaver1990matrix,kassimali2012matrix}{}{}]}.
\par Since displacements corresponding to rigid body translations or infinitesimal rotations do not incur any internal loading, the elastic stiffness matrix is not full-rank, but of rank $6n^{\mathrm{jt}}-6$.
To fully define the problem of relating displacements with forces and moments, displacements are required to amount to zero rigid body translation and rotation or, equivalently, $\bm{v}\in\mathrm{null}\left(\bm{K}_{\kappa}\right)^{\perp}$.
This constraint is represented by
\begin{equation}\sum_{i=0}^{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\bm{s}_{i}=\bm{0},\ \sum_{i=0}^{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\bm{j}_{i}\times\bm{s}_{i}+\bm{r}_{i}=\bm{0}\end{equation}and can be simplified to $\bm{H}_{\kappa}\bm{v}=\bm{0}$, where $\bm{v}$ is as defined in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:structures:aggregated_vec} and $\bm{H}_{\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}^{6\times 6n^{\mathrm{jt}}}$ is the matrix that relates generalized displacements with rigid body translation and rotational displacements.
This matrix can be seen as an orthogonal projection on $\mathrm{null}\left(\bm{K}_{\kappa}\right)$.
Since $\bm{K}_{\kappa}$ is symmetric, $\mathrm{null}\left(\bm{K}_{\kappa}\right)^{\perp}=,whichmeansthateveryfeasibleloadvector$\bm{P}$mustalsoverify$\bm{H}_{\kappa} \bm{P} = \bm{0}$.Thisfeasibilityconditioncanbeverifiedbyreplacingdisplacementsbyloadsin\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:structures:displacement_constraint},whichleadstothenaturalconstraintthatsumofforcesandmomentsarezero:\begin{equation}\sum_{i=0}^{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\bm{f}_{i}=\bm{0},\ \sum_{i=0}^{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\bm{j}_{i}\times\bm{f}_{i}+\bm{m}_{i}=\bm{0}.\end{equation}\par Theseconstraintsontheloadsanddisplacementsensurethattherelationshipbetweenthemisone-to-one.Infact,since$\bm{v} \in , the inverse relationship of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:structures:elastic_stiffness} is provided directly by
\begin{equation}\bm{v}={\bm{K}_{\kappa}}^{+}\bm{K}_{\kappa}\bm{v}={\bm{K}_{\kappa}}^{+}\bm{P}.\end{equation}\par During the assembly of the elastic stiffness matrix, a member stiffness matrix, which gives the relationship between frame displacements and member loads, is also computed.
Computing the loads applied to the individual members is essential to estimate material stresses that could result in local failure.
For a member $i$, $\bm{l}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{6}$ is defined as the vector containing the axial force, shear forces, torsional moment, and bending moments it is subject to in its local coordinate frame.
The member loads can be aggregated into the vector
\begin{equation}\bm{F}:=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{l}_{0}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{l}_{n^{\mathrm{mb}}-1}\end{bmatrix}.\end{equation}A first-order approximation of the linear relationship between external displacements and member loads results in the member stiffness matrix $\bm{K}^{\mathrm{mb}}_{\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}^{6n^{\mathrm{mb}}\times 6n^{\mathrm{jt}}}$.
This matrix can be assembled in a process similar to the assembly of $\bm{K}_{\kappa}$~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{kassimali2012matrix}{}{}]} that can be performed in parallel.
With this definition, the following holds:
\begin{equation}\bm{F}=\bm{K}^{\mathrm{mb}}_{\kappa}\bm{v}.\end{equation}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Structural performance}
\par Now that the basis for the matrix structural analysis of modular configurations has been laid out, structural performance indicators of arbitrary configurations are defined.
Two of them as specified explicitly in the following.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Indicators based on stiffness}
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Maximum compliance}
\par For a matrix $\bm{M}$, let $\sigma^{\max}(\bm{M})$ be the maximum singular value of $\bm{M}$.
It is well known that
\begin{equation*}\sigma^{\max}(\bm{M})=\max_{\lVert\bm{x}\rVert_{2}=1}{\lVert\bm{M}\bm{x}\rVert_{2}}.\end{equation*}Based on this property of singular values and \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:structures:elastic_displacements_sol}, a possible structural performance indicator of $\kappa$ is $\sigma^{\max}({\bm{K}_{\kappa}}^{+})$.
This value represents the maximum norm that a displacement vector resulting from a unit-norm force vector can take.
It is therefore a worst-case scenario that should be sought as small as possible to ensure that a structure is stiff for all arbitrary loading conditions.
\par A drawback of this performance indicator is that it accounts for load vectors that include both forces and moments, two physical quantities with different units, and has therefore little meaning as such.
The same can be said regarding displacement vectors, which include translations and rotations.
Therefore, a more meaningful performance indicator relies on pure translational displacements that result from external forces only.
Based on \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:structures:aggregated_vec} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{eq:structures:elastic_stiffness}, the relationship between translational displacements and external forces is
\begin{equation}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{s}_{0}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{s}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\bm{K}_{\kappa}}^{+}{\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{f}_{0}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{f}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}\end{bmatrix},\end{equation}where
\begin{equation}\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}=\bm{I}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}}\otimes{\begin{bmatrix}\bm{I}_{3}&\bm{0}_{3\times 3}\end{bmatrix}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3n^{\mathrm{jt}}\times 6n^{\mathrm{jt}}}\end{equation}and where $\otimes$ is the Kronecker product.
These considerations lead to the performance indicator
\begin{equation}\sigma_{1}:=\sigma^{\max}\left(\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}{\bm{K}_{\kappa}}^{+}{\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}}^{\top}\right).\end{equation}Since $\sigma^{\max}\left(\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}{\bm{K}_{\kappa}}^{+}{\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}}^{\top}\right)$ is a square symmetric matrix, $\sigma_{1}$ is in fact an eigenvalue and its associated eigenvector of norm one maximizes translational displacements.
Note that three other similar performance measures could be defined, to consider all potential impacts of external forces and moments on translational and rotational displacements.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Maximum member axial load}
\par Another indicator of performance pertains to the maximum axial load present in a structural frame subject to unit-norm external forces.
The estimation of members' axial loads is crucial, since local buckling, when the compressive axial load in a member exceeds its Euler's critical load, is a common mode of failure of framed structures.
Based on \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:structures:elastic_displacements_sol}, \lx@cref{refnum}{eq:structures:member_loads_vec}, \lx@cref{refnum}{eq:structures:member_elastic_stiffness}, and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{eq:structures:translation_only}, assuming without loss of generality that each member load vector $\bm{l}_{i}$ contains the member's axial load as its first component, the axial load $l^{\mathrm{ax}}_{i}$ of member $i$ is given by
\begin{equation}l^{\mathrm{ax}}_{i}:=\bm{M}^{\mathrm{ax}}_{i}\bm{L}=\bm{M}^{\mathrm{ax}}_{i}\bm{K}^{\mathrm{mb}}_{\kappa}{\bm{K}}^{+}{\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}}^{\top}\bm{P}^{\mathrm{tr}},\end{equation}where
\begin{equation}\bm{M}^{\mathrm{ax}}_{i}:={\bm{e}_{i}^{n^{\mathrm{mb}}}}^{\top}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix},\end{equation}with $\bm{e}_{i}^{n^{\mathrm{mb}}}$ being the vector of size $n^{\mathrm{mb}}$ with the $i$-th entry equal to $1$ and the others equal to $0$.
Since $\bm{M}^{\mathrm{ax}}_{i}\bm{K}^{\mathrm{mb}}_{\kappa}{\bm{K}}^{+}{\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}}^{\top}$ is a row matrix, the maximum value reached when it is multiplied on the right by a unit-norm vector is its euclidean norm.
As a consequence, a performance indicator equal to the maximum axial load under unit-norm external forces is defined by
\begin{equation}\sigma_{2}:=\max_{i\in\llbracket 0;n^{\mathrm{mb}}-1\rrbracket}\lVert\bm{M}^{\mathrm{ax}}_{i}\bm{K}^{\mathrm{mb}}_{\kappa}{\bm{K}_{\kappa}}^{+}{\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}}^{\top}\rVert_{2}.\end{equation}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Numerical comparison of modular configurations}

}}}\@add@centering\end{figure}\par The structural performance of six modular configurations was evaluated against the indicators of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{subsec:structures:structural_performance}.
The first four analyzed configurations are the standard quad-rotorcraft, a quad-rotorcraft in the shape of a tetrahedron (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:prototype:confs:tetra}), a 4-by-4 flat array, and a 16-module tetrahedron formed by combining four tetrahedron-shaped quad-rotorcraft (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:prototype:confs:tetragen2}).
These configurations are chosen since they correspond to two pairs of configurations with the same number of modules.
For the sake of completeness, the two other configurations that were flight tested, that is the standard hexarotor (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:prototype:confs:hexa}) and a 10-module tetrahedron (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:prototype:confs:decatetra}), are also added to the results.
Results are summarized in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{table:structures:performance_comparison} and the corresponding external loads for the standard quad-rotorcraft configuration are represented in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:structures:deformed_quad:max_compliance} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{fig:structures:deformed_quad:max_ax_load}.
It can be shown that for an equal number of modules, configurations with more layers have a lower value for the introduced performance indicators, which corresponds to a better performance.
\par\begin{table}\begin{center}\begin{tabular}[]{l c c c c}Name&Modules&Layers&$\sigma_{1}$&$\sigma_{2}$\\
Quadrotor&4&1&1&1\\
Tetrahedron Quadrotor&4&2&0.265&0.764\\
16-module Array&16&1&8.741&1.361\\
Tetrahedron Hexadecarotor&16&4&1.391&1.071\\
Hexarotor&6&1&1.825&1.097\\
Tetrahedron Decarotor&10&2&0.544&0.831\\
\end{tabular}\end{center}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{I}}{Structural performance indicators evaluated against different configurations.
For the first pairs of configurations, the number of modules is chosen to represent comparable configurations and the number of layers corresponds to the different heights module can be at in a configuration, $1$ being a flat configuration.
}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small TABLE I}}{\small Structural performance indicators evaluated against different configurations.
For the first pairs of configurations, the number of modules is chosen to represent comparable configurations and the number of layers corresponds to the different heights module can be at in a configuration, $1$ being a flat configuration.
}}}\end{table}\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Configuration optimization}
\par The programs introduced in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:control} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{sec:structures} are meant to be used on pre-determined modular configurations.
Given the versatility offered by the modular architecture presented in this work and the high number of feasible configurations, it is desirable to design methods to optimize configurations based on their structural and control properties.
Using MIP, it is possible to describe configurations and their properties with binary decision variables, such that the programs already introduced can be extended to account for a parameterized space of possible configurations.
In this section, a MIP formulation of feasible configurations and their relevant properties is provided.
Binary variables indicate whether a module is allocated to a configuration at a given position, with a given orientation, and with a given rotor spinning direction.
The fact that modules must form a connected entity is expressed as a set of linear constraints based on a MIP formulation of contiguity for graphs~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{shirabe2005model}{}{}]}.
Constraints to prevent configurations resulting in modules in the wake of other modules are also introduced.
Furthermore, most linear constraints employed in previous programs for control allocation or structural optimization, which become bilinear under the assumption of a parameterized configuration space, are shown to be linearizable with the ``big-M'' method, a standard method of linear programming for linearizing products of variables of which at most one is non-binary~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{hillier2015introduction}{}{}]}.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Graph of admissible module positions}
\par The set of positions that modules may take in a configuration and whether a connection exists between modules placed at a pair of positions represents a graph defined in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:optimal:graph}.
\begin{definition}$G_{\infty}=(V_{\infty},E_{\infty})$ is the infinite undirected graph (a graph with an infinite number of vertices~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{diestel2024graph}{}{}]}) where
\IEEEitemize\itemize@item@$V_{\infty}=\mathcal{P}$, as defined in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:modular:positions_group},
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@for $\bm{x}$ and $\bm{y}\in\mathcal{P}$, the unordered pair $\{\bm{x},\bm{y}\}$ is in $E_{\infty}$ if and only if $\bm{x}-\bm{y}$ is in $\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{tr}}$, as defined in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:modular:cube_connections}.
\endIEEEitemize\end{definition}\par With this definition, the fact that $G_{\infty}$ is undirected is justified by the fact that $\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{tr}}$ is stable by negation.
The positions in a configuration can be described as a subgraph of $G_{\infty}$ of size $n$, but to perform numerical optimization of configurations, $G_{\infty}$ needs to be reduced to a finite graph.
Increasing finite subgraphs of $G_{\infty}$ are defined for this reason in the following.
\begin{definition}The increasing sequence $G_{i}=(V_{i},E_{i}),\ i\in\mathbb{N}$ is defined by induction to be
\@@amsalign
\end{definition}\par\begin{fact}For a valid configuration $\kappa=\{(\bm{p}_{i},\bm{\eta}_{i},\epsilon_{i}),\ i\in\llbracket 0;n-1\rrbracket\}$, there is a subgraph of $G_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}$ with vertices $(\bm{x}_{0},\dots,\bm{x}_{n-1})$ and a vector $\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}$ such that
\begin{equation*}\forall i,\in\llbracket 0;n-1\rrbracket\ \bm{p}_{i}=\bm{x}_{i}+\bm{z}.\end{equation*}\end{fact}\@proof Knowing that all the modules in a valid configuration must be connected, there exists one module from which every other module of the configuration can be reached with at most $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ connections.
Denoting this module's index $j\in\llbracket 0;n-1\rrbracket$, then, by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:optimal:finite_graphs}, $\{p_{i}-p_{j}\ |\ i\in\llbracket 0;n-1\rrbracket\}$ is a subset of $G_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}$, such that the resulting subgraph and $\bm{z}=\bm{p}_{j}$ are as stated.
\qed\end@proof
\par\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fact:optimal:subgraph_size} ensures that when searching for an optimal configuration of $n$ modules, using the finite graph $G_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}$ instead of $G_{\infty}$ is not restrictive.
In the rest of this section, $G=(V,E)$ is assumed to be a finite subgraph of $G_{\infty}$ of $N$ vertices appropriate for the problems formulated.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Mixed-integer representation of configurations}
\par\begin{definition}Let $\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}$ be a variable that represents the offset vector as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:modular:configuration}.
For $\bm{x}\in V$, $\bm{\eta}\in\mathcal{H}$, $\epsilon\in\{0,1\}$, let
\IEEEitemize\itemize@item@$\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}}\in\{0,1\}$ indicate whether a module is placed at position $\bm{z}+\bm{x}$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\chi_{\bm{x}\eta}^{\mathrm{po}}\in\{0,1\}$ indicate whether a module is placed at position $\bm{z}+\bm{x}$ with orientation $\bm{\eta}$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\chi_{\bm{x}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{pd}}\in\{0,1\}$ indicate whether a module is placed at position $\bm{z}+\bm{x}$ with rotor spinning direction $\epsilon$.
\endIEEEitemize\end{definition}\par The variables $\bm{z}$, $\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}}$, $\chi_{\bm{x}\eta}^{\mathrm{po}}$, and $\chi_{\bm{x}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{pd}}$ completely describe modular configurations.
Based on these variables' definition, the information of allocated positions is redundant, which is a deliberate choice to simplify subsequent definitions.
To ensure that exactly one orientation and one spinning direction is chosen per allocated position, and zero per unallocated position, the introduced binary variables must verify
\@@amsalign
Moreover, for $\bm{z}$ to satisfy \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:modular:configuration}, the following equality is required
\begin{equation}\bm{z}\sum_{\bm{x}\in V}\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}}+\sum_{\bm{x}\in V}\bm{x}\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}}=\bm{0}.\end{equation}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Formulation of the connectivity constraint}
\par A configuration described by a subgraph of $G$ is valid only if that subgraph is connected.
A MIP formulation can be used to define this constraint.
It is based on the work presented in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{shirabe2005model}{}{}]} for allocation of contiguous spatial units.
That work introduces a flow formulation where an ``artificial sink'' is chosen among the possible units and all the allocated units must be able to route their flow to the ``artificial sink'' via other connected allocated units.
Assuming that the number of modules $n\in\mathbb{N}$ for a desired configuration is fixed, the MIP contiguity constraint can be formulated by first introducing the variables:
\IEEEitemize\itemize@item@$\gamma_{\bm{x}}$, a binary variable to indicate whether $\bm{x}\in V$ is the sink,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\delta_{\bm{x}\bm{y}}$, a nonnegative real variable that represents the flow from $\bm{x}$ to $\bm{y}$ for $\{\bm{x},\bm{y}\}\in E$.
\endIEEEitemize Then, set of allocated positions are ensured contiguous with:
\@@amsalign
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Wake interactions}
\par The search for optimal configurations should avoid returning solutions whose modules are directly in the wake of another module, as wake interactions would reduce the efficiency of these modules.
This requirement can be enforced by adding the constraints
\begin{equation}\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}}\leq 1-\chi_{{\bm{y}}\eta}^{\mathrm{po}}\end{equation}for every $\bm{x}\in V$, $\bm{y}\in V$, $\bm{\eta}\in\mathcal{H}$ such that a module at position $\bm{x}$ is in the wake of a module at position $\bm{y}$ with orientation $\bm{\eta}$.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Control allocation matrix}
\par To simultaneously optimize configurations and their control allocation matrix, the variable $\bm{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times 6}$ is defined.
Unlike in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:control}, $\bm{C}$ has as many rows as vertices in $V$, with each one corresponding to a possible module position.
$V$ is therefore given an arbitrary indexing, so that its vertices are written $\{\bm{x}_{i}\ |\ i\in\llbracket 0;N-1\rrbracket\}$.
\par The matrix ${\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tt}}}$ is also modified accordingly, and is now of size $6\times N$.
Adapting \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{fact:modular:thrust_torque_matrices} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{def:control:thrust_torque_matrix} leads to
{
\@@amsalign
}
\par For $\bm{C}$ to be a proper control allocation matrix, the equalities of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fact:control:allocation_matrix} must be satisfied.
$\bm{C}\bm{H}=\bm{C}$ remains a linear equality in the variables as long as $\bm{H}$ is fixed.
Fixing $\bm{H}$ makes perfect sense because it determines the degrees of freedom of the vehicle to optimize.
Meanwhile, ${\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tt}}}\bm{C}=\bm{H}$ is not linear anymore since ${\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tt}}}$ depends on the parameterized configuration, but it can be linearized via the ``big-M'' method as shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fact:optimal:mc_lin}.
\par\begin{fact}${\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tt}}}\bm{C}=\bm{H}$ is equivalent to an equality equation written as a sum of products, where each product involves at most one non-binary variable, allowing it to be linearized with the ``big-M'' method.
\end{fact}\@proof From \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:optimal:var_thrust_torque}, it can be seen that the only non-binary variables in the equation ${\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tt}}}\bm{C}=\bm{H}$ are $\bm{z}$ and $\bm{C}$ itself.
By multiplying both sides by $\sum_{\bm{x}\in V}\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}}$ (which is a positive term), \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:optimal:offset} can be used to substitute $\bm{z}\sum_{\bm{x}\in V}\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}}$ with binary variables only, leaving the coefficients of $\bm{C}$ as the only non-binary variables in the resulting equation.
\qed\end@proof
\par Now that the constraints on $\bm{C}$ have been shown to be representable as linear, the same constraints as the ones in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prog:control:alloc_max_auth_2} may be integrated to a configuration optimization problem.
For example, a program can be formulated to maximize the yaw control authority of a 6DOF hexa-rotorcraft.
Control authority can also be used as a constraint, to enforce a minimum authority along particular dimensions, while optimizing a different type of objective, for example a structural one.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Structural optimization}
\par The structural optimization of modular configurations follows the stiffness formulation detailed in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:structures}.
The case considered is the one of ensuring robust constraints of the type
\begin{equation}\lVert\bm{P}\rVert_{2}\leq 1\implies\bm{c}^{\top}\bm{v}\leq\lambda,\end{equation}where $\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{6n^{\mathrm{jt}}}$ is either fixed or a variable, $\bm{v}$ and $\bm{P}$ are valid displacements and loads related by the stiffness equation, and $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$ is a variable.
This type of constraint can be integrated to MIP programs via a reformulation of the worst-case scenario as second-order conic constraints.\par To do so, an extended list of joints from all the positions in $V$ is defined with
\begin{equation}\mathcal{J}_{V}=[\bm{j}_{0},\dots,\bm{j}_{n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1}],\end{equation}where $n^{\mathrm{jt}}$ denotes the total number of joints and $\forall i\in\llbracket 0;n^{\mathrm{jt}}-1\rrbracket,\ \bm{j}_{i}\in\{\bm{x}_{j}+\bm{g}\ |\ j\in\llbracket 0;N-1\rrbracket,\bm{g}\in V^{\mathcal{D}}\}$.
Note that the offset vector $\bm{z}$ is not required, since a common translation of a space frame's joints does not change its stiffness.
The elastic stiffness matrix of the structure $\bm{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{6n^{\mathrm{jt}}\times 6n^{\mathrm{jt}}}$ can be represented as
\begin{equation}\bm{K}=\sum_{\bm{x}\in V}\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}}\bm{k}_{\bm{x}},\end{equation}where $\bm{k}_{\bm{x}}$ is the contribution to the global stiffness of the members belonging to the module placed at $\bm{x}$.
\par In general, the rank of $\bm{K}$ may not be equal to $6n^{\mathrm{jt}}-6$ anymore, since the columns and rows of $\bm{K}$ corresponding to joints of unallocated modules are zero.
However, valid displacements and loads can still be defined as the ones that are orthogonal to the null space of $\bm{K}$, similarly to the constraints of \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:structures:displacement_constraint} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{eq:structures:load_constraint}, implying that these displacements and loads verify
\@@amsalign
\par To simplify \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:optimal:pb_def}, the following problem can be solved:
\begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{maximize}&\bm{c}^{\top}\bm{v}\\
\text{subject to}&\lVert\bm{P}\rVert_{2}\leq 1\\
&\bm{K}\bm{v}=\bm{P}\\
&\bm{K}^{+}\bm{P}=\bm{v}\end{array}.\end{equation*}This problem is a convex quadratic one, whose optimal value is equal to $\lVert\bm{K}^{+}\bm{c}\rVert_{2}$ and reached for $\bm{v}=\frac{{(\bm{K}^{+})}^{2}\bm{c}}{\lVert\bm{K}^{+}\bm{c}\rVert_{2}}$.
\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:optimal:pb_def} is then replaced by
\@@amsalign
where $\bm{w}$ is a newly introduced displacement variable, which, when verifying \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:optimal:struct_w:1} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{eq:optimal:struct_w:2}, always guarantees that $\lVert\bm{K}^{+}\bm{c}\rVert_{2}\leq\lambda$.
When added to a configuration optimization problem, this formulation results in a problem that is at least a MISOCP (MIP second-order cone) problem, owing to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:optimal:struct_w:2}.
A possible application of this type of inequality is to ensure that the member axial loads are minimized when subject to unit-norm load vectors.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Example}
\par As an example, a program is formulated to maximize the control authority in terms of three-dimensional torques for an arbitrary a 6DOF configuration of $12$ modules.
The projection of actuation vectors on the space of torques only is given by the operator
\begin{equation*}\bm{S}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{0}_{3\times 3}&\bm{0}_{3\times 3}\\
\bm{0}_{3\times 3}&\bm{I}_{3}\end{bmatrix}.\end{equation*}Therefore, the optimal configuration is found by solving the following program, which results in the previously studied configuration shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:control:optimized:1}.
This optimization problem was formulated using the Matlab package YALMIP~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{yalmip}{}{}]} and solved with Gurobi~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{gurobi}{}{}]}.
\par\par\begin{program}With variables $\lambda$, $\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}}$, $\chi_{\bm{x}\eta}^{\mathrm{po}}$, $\chi_{\bm{x}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{pd}}$, $\bm{z}$, $\delta_{\bm{x}}$, $\gamma_{\bm{x}\bm{y}}$, and intermediate variables created from the ``big-M'' method:
\begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{minimize}&\lambda\\
\text{subject to}&\\
&\kern-20.00003pt{\bm{M}^{\mathrm{tt}}}\bm{C}=\bm{H}\\
&\kern-20.00003pt\bm{C}\bm{H}=\bm{C}\\
&\kern-20.00003pt\lVert\bm{S}\bm{C}^{\top}\bm{A}_{i,:}^{\top}\rVert_{2}\leq s\bm{b}_{i}-s\bm{A}_{i,:}\bm{u}^{\mathrm{h}},\ \forall i\in\llbracket 0;2n-1\rrbracket\\
&\kern-20.00003pt\chi_{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{p}},\chi_{\bm{x}\eta}^{\mathrm{po}},\chi_{\bm{x}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{pd}},\bm{z},\gamma_{\bm{x}},\delta_{\bm{x}\bm{y}}\text{\ verify \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:optimal:compatible_orientations}, \lx@cref{refnum}{eq:optimal:compatible_directions}, \lx@cref{refnum}{eq:optimal:offset}, \lx@cref{refnum}{eq:optimal:contiguous:1}, \lx@cref{refnum}{eq:optimal:contiguous:2}, \lx@cref{refnum}{eq:optimal:contiguous:3}, and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{eq:optimal:wake}}.\end{array}\end{equation*}\end{program}\par\par\par\par\par\par\begin{figure}\centering\includegraphics[width]{module}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{14}}{Module prototype.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Fig. 14}}{\small Module prototype.}}}\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{The module prototype}
\par A prototype of the Dodecacopter module was built to assess the practical feasibility of the concept presented in this work (\lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:prototype:module}, \lx@cref{refnum}{fig:prototype:corner}, and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{fig:prototype:confs}).
The components of the prototype are discussed in this section and a few configurations that were flight-tested are presented.
A comparison of the required average motor inputs to hover the flown configurations is performed with the goal of assessing the impact of rotor-rotor interactions on performance for three-dimensional configurations.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Prototype description}
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Frame and connectors}
\par The frame of the prototype is made of carbon fiber reinforced polymer (CRFP) tubes connected together with multiple 3D printed plastic parts.
The first plastic part is a short tube with a closed end made of soft thermal polyurethane (TPU).
These tubes enclose each end of the CRFP tubes and remain in place with a tight fit.
A pair of 3D printed matching parts are used for each corner of the dodecahedron frame.
Each pair is joined together with metal fasteners and clamps three TPU end tubes.
Three assembled corners forming a connection between three modules are shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:prototype:corner}.
\par\begin{figure}\centering\includegraphics[width]{connectors}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{15}}{Three corners belonging to different modules forming a connection.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Fig. 15}}{\small Three corners belonging to different modules forming a connection.}}}\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Propulsion}\ \par For propulsion, each module prototype uses a brushless motor with a rating of $465\text{\,}\mathrm{kV}$ controlled by an electronic speed controller, and a three-blade propeller with a diameter of about $39.3\text{\,}\mathrm{cm}$ that is oriented either CW or CCW.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Flight controller}
\par Each module can hold a Pixhawk 4 flight controller that sends a PWM signal to the electronic speed controller and may be used as well to control other modules of the assembly via standard servo cable connections.
Only one flight controller per configuration was used to control the whole vehicle for most flights.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Communications}
\par The prototype includes a $900\text{\,}\mathrm{MHz}$ radio receiver to receive radio commands from the operator and a $2.4\text{\,}\mathrm{GHz}$ ESP8266 WiFi module to communicate with the ground station.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Power source}
\par Two slots are reserved per module to place six-cell lithium-polymer batteries with a capacity of $1350\text{\,}\mathrm{mA}\text{\,}\mathrm{h}$.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Tested configurations}
\par The configurations that were flown are summarized in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{table:prototype:configurations}.
The ArduPilot flight controller software~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ardupilot}{}{}]} was used to control the vehicles.
Each configuration required tuning the gains of the control loops manually, which was particularly time-consuming.
In the future, methods to automatically tune the different control loops based on the assembled configuration would be desirable.
The standard configurations, that is the quadrotor and hexarotor, were the easiest to tune.
\par The tetrahedron configurations were flown successfully as well, but suffered from a slightly reduced power efficiency from wake interactions, since some overlap between rotors at different heights occurs, as shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:prototype:tetrahedron_top_views}.
The 4-module tetrahedron configuration was also characterized by a relatively low yaw control authority, due to the particular rotor arrangement and the resulting unique control allocation matrix, which implies that the top motor is issued control input changes three times as high as the other motors to control yaw, as seen from the control allocation matrix:
\begin{equation}\bm{C}^{\mathrm{tetraquad}}\propto\begin{bmatrix}1&-1&3&3\\
1&-1&-1&-1\\
1&1&-3&-1\\
1&1&1&-1\end{bmatrix}.\end{equation}The effect of the magnitude differences between coefficients assigned to different modules can be seen in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:prototype:hover_plots:tetra}.
This configuration had therefore a tendency to start spinning around the yaw axis and show difficulty to recover.
The larger tetrahedron-shaped configurations did not show the same issue, and their overactuated nature ensured that the control allocation matrix could be chosen to yield a good control authority around all moment axes.
\par The 6DOF hexarotor also posed a challenge from the difficulty in tuning its control loops and from its reduced thrust-to-weight ratio, leading to the motors running close to maximum thrust at hover and leaving little room for control.
At $1.7\text{\,}\mathrm{kg}$ per module, the Dodecacopter prototype vehicles are relatively heavy for their size, which clearly limits possibilities for creating 6DOF vehicles capable of agile flights.
This heavy weight is the direct consequence of the complex frame geometry and required number of attachment points which, when manufactured with rapid prototyping methods, need to be somewhat bulky to be structurally sound.
Nonetheless, the successful flights of very diverse configurations of up to sixteen modules show the versatility of the introduced modular system.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(e)}}{Tetrahedron Decarotor.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(e)}}{\small Tetrahedron Decarotor.}}}\@add@centering\end{subfigure}\begin{subfigure}[165.59853pt]\centering\includegraphics[width]{tetragen2}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(f)}}{Tetrahedron Hexadecarotor.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(f)}}{\small Tetrahedron Hexadecarotor.}}}\@add@centering\end{subfigure}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{16}}{}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Fig. 16}}{\small}}}\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Thrust efficiency of three-dimensional configurations}
\par To evaluate the impact that stacked layers of rotors have on the total thrust produced by a vehicle, motor inputs required to sustain hover were recorded and compared for the hexarotor, tetrahedron quadrotor, and tetrahedron hexadecarotor.
Vehicles were hovered during at least $30\text{\,}\mathrm{s}$ each with a motion capture system to provide position feedback.
The average of the normalized motor inputs over all motors and hovering time are shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{table:prototype:configurations} and samples of flight data are shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:prototype:hover_plots}.
Vehicles with rotor overlap required a higher average motor input to hover of about $14\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$.
However there was no significant difference between the three tetrahedron configurations despite their different numbers of layers.
Although no definitive conclusion can be made based solely on this observation, it seems to indicate that the loss in efficiency is mostly due to rotor wake interactions between modules at adjacent layers and that interactions between layers further from each other are small.
This suggests that larger three-dimensional configurations with multiple layers and limited propulsive efficiency losses are possible, as long as the positioning of the modules avoids direct rotor overlap.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(b)}}{Tetrahedron Quadrotor.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(b)}}{\small Tetrahedron Quadrotor.}}}\@add@centering\end{subfigure}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{18}}{Control inputs and attitude during hover.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Fig. 18}}{\small Control inputs and attitude during hover.}}}\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusion}
\par A new class of modular UAS based on a module shaped as a regular dodecahedron was presented in this paper.
Specifically, this UAS belongs to the category of systems composed of multiple rotorcraft modules which are meant to be assembled into vehicles of varying shapes, sizes, and payload capacity.
The uniqueness of the introduced system resides in the chosen module shape and the associated connection method between modules.
It was shown that these innovations result in a large variety of achievable geometries of aerial vehicles.
Not only the modules can be assembled to recreate conventional multirotor configurations and flat arrays of rotors, they can also assemble in three dimensions to create stiff structures while limiting wake interactions, or be oriented at different angles to grant six degrees of freedom to an assembled vehicle.
The potential for a large number of rotors in a vehicle implies a large space of possible control allocation matrices.
New methods for the computation of optimal allocation matrices based on control authority or power consumption were introduced and numerically evaluated on a configuration example.
It is worth noting that these methods can be applied to non-modular vehicles with many rotors as well.
To support the claim that three-dimensional module arrangements enable stiffer and stronger configurations, the structure of modular vehicles was represented as a space frame and analyzed using the elastic stiffness method.
It was shown that the intuition behind the better structural properties of three-dimensional vehicles is justified by considering indicators of structural performance on different configurations.
These indicators are based on a robust formulation of the frame displacements and its internal loads when subject to arbitrary bounded loads.
In addition, it was shown that the configuration optimization of modular vehicles can be performed with mixed-integer programming, owing to the discreteness of the set of configuration parameters.
Relevant measures of vehicle performance based on their control allocation and structural properties were reformulated to yield programs with linear or second-order conic constraints, for which efficient solvers exist.
Finally, a module prototype that was designed, built, and flown in six different configurations of up to sixteen modules was described.
This prototype proved the feasibility of the designed modular concept and provided insights on the challenges that some configurations might pose.
\par Several directions remain open to continue the work on this system, other modular systems, and highly overactuated multirotor vehicles in general.
Regarding the control of modular vehicles, a fully decentralized control system would be desirable to ensure a high level of robustness of the system.
The presented prototype was controlled in a centralized fashion, since the focus was placed on the control allocation problem, but this would become impractical for a larger number of modules than what has been tested so far.
Decentralized control would also be a necessity for the flexible rearrangement of modular vehicles, for example through autonomous de- and self-assembly.
Some of the authors have already started looking at the issue of controlling a different type of modular aerial vehicle in which modules do not communicate and are unaware of their position in the vehicle~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{shahab2022controlling}{}{}]}.
The presented control allocation strategies themselves would benefit from a comparison under experimental conditions.
It is expected however that a large number of rotors would be required to see a significant difference in, for example, power consumption.
Then, to enable the control of arbitrary configurations without flight controller tuning, it would be necessary to develop methods to infer optimal control parameters for new configurations.
A possible avenue for doing so would be to perform a system's identification with a few small vehicle configurations, to then use the identified models to derive the models of arbitrary configurations, and finally to deduce optimal control parameters from model-based tuning methods.
However, it remains unclear how some effects might contribute to the dynamics of some specific, large configurations.
For examples, the wake interactions that occur with modules at different heights or different orientations are hard to model and quantify.
They therefore require extensive study to understand how they might affect large vehicle configurations, an other area that the authors have been exploring~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{epps2022wakeinteractions}{}{}]}.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Convexity of power consumption-based optimization}
\par\par Let $f:(0,\infty)\mapsto\mathbb{R}$ be the function defined by
\begin{equation}\forall x\in(0,\infty)\ f(x)=x^{-\frac{3}{4}}\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}\left(-\frac{3}{4},\frac{1}{2},-x\right),\end{equation}where $\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}$ is the hypergeometric function of the first kind.
From~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DLMF}{}{}, Eq.~13.2.2]}, $\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}\left(a,b,z\right)$ is given by
\begin{equation}\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}\left(a,b,z\right)=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{s}}{(b)_{s}s!}z^{s}=1+\frac{a}{b}z+\frac{a(a+1)}{b(b+1)2!}z^{2}+\dots~.\end{equation}from which it can be shown that
\begin{equation}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z}\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}\left(a,b,z\right)=\frac{a}{b}\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}\left(a+1,b+1,z\right).\end{equation}In addition, Kummer's transformation~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DLMF}{}{}, Eq.~13.2.39]} gives
\begin{equation}\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}\left(a,b,z\right)=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{e}^{z}\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}\left(b-a,b,-z\right).\end{equation}By substituting in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:app:convexity:4} the values $a=-\frac{3}{4}$ and $b=\frac{1}{2}$,
\begin{equation}f(x)=x^{-\frac{3}{4}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{e}^{x}\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}\left(\frac{5}{4},\frac{1}{2},x\right).\end{equation}Consequently, using \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:app:convexity:3},
\@@amsalign
which, after substituting the values of $\prescript{}{1}{F}_{1}$ with their power series expansion and grouping the terms of same power together, leads to

\par\par\par hence $f$ is convex on $(0,\infty)$.
